He passed on a few years back, God rest him, but I swear to you, Doc, I hear him out on the front porch. Clear as a bell. He'll call out my name, just exactly the way he used to when he'd come over to see if I wanted to sit a spell or walk down to the hardware store. Just calls my name right through the screen door. \n\nAnd if he ain't doing that, he's humming. He used to sing in the church choir—had this real deep, nice voice, you know, always stood in the back row on Sundays—and I will hear him humming one of those old choir songs out there. It is so clear, so loud and real, it is exactly like he is standing right there on the porch waiting on me. I even get up out of my chair sometimes, when my knees aren't bothering me too bad, and I go look out the front window. I'll pull the curtain back just a peek, fully expecting to see him leaning against the wooden railing in his good hat. But there is never anybody out there. Just the empty porch and the street. It is a peculiar thing, hearing him so plain like that when the house is quiet, but it is just so clear...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recent changes in your memory, your ability to focus, or your ability to keep track of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, my memory ain't what it used to be, I'll grant you that, but I'm 84 years old, so I reckon that's to be expected, ain't it? But as for focusing... well, that's a whole other kettle of fish lately. It's like my mind just can't sit still, no matter how hard I try to make it behave. \n\nJust the other day, I was sitting in my armchair, trying to read the church paper. Pastor leaves it in the mailbox every Tuesday, and I like to read the announcements, see who's sick or who had a new grandbaby, you know how it is. So I'm sitting there, got my reading glasses on, and I'm trying to read this little article about the upcoming bake sale. And my wife, she's in the kitchen, making up a batch of iced tea, and I'm trying to tell her about the bake sale, but then... well, a car drives by outside. Not even a loud car, just a regular old sedan, but the sound of those tires on the pavement, it just grabs me. And I look up, and I'm watching the car go down the street, and then I see a blue jay land on the telephone wire. And I'm looking at that blue jay, thinking about how they're such mean birds, always chasing the smaller ones away from the feeder, and then the radio in the kitchen catches my ear. It's just playing a commercial for that furniture store over in the next town, the one with the catchy little jingle, and suddenly I'm humming the jingle. \n\nAnd my wife, she comes in with the iced tea, and she says, \"Well, what about the bake sale?\" And I just look at her, Doc. I look right at her, and I have absolutely no earthly idea what she's talking about. I'd completely forgotten the church paper was even in my lap. I forgot I was reading it, I forgot I was talking to her... it's like my brain just gets pulled this way and that way, pulled by every little sound or movement, like a leaf in a strong wind. A bird, a car, a song on the radio... it just yanks me right away from whatever I'm doing, and I can't for the life of me remember what I was about to say or do. It's been happening more and more lately, and it's frustrating, I tell you. It's mighty frustrating to not be able to hold a thought in your head long enough to finish a sentence.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any times, perhaps when you are doing your grocery shopping or managing your household bills, where you find it difficult to complete tasks that you used to handle easily?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, see, my wife handles the checkbook. She always has, ever since I was working full-time. She's just always been better with the numbers, making sure the light bill and the water bill get mailed off on time. And she usually does the grocery shopping on Wednesday mornings because that is when the Piggly Wiggly does the senior discount. But as for other things... things I used to do all on my own without even thinking twice about them... well, I suppose it has been getting a lot harder lately. \n\nTake my garden, for example. I have always kept a nice vegetable patch out in the backyard. Grown the best Beefsteak tomatoes in the neighborhood, if I do say so myself, along with bush beans, and cucumbers, and summer squash. I used to be out there every single morning, soon as the sun was up, with my trowel and my old straw hat, pulling up the crabgrass and checking the leaves for bugs. But this year... well, I have just let parts of it go completely. I will stand there at the kitchen sink with my coffee, looking out the window, and I can see the weeds growing up knee-high by the back fence where the cucumbers are supposed to be. And I know I ought to go out there and tend to it, but I just... I don't. I can't seem to get the gumption to do it. It just feels like a mountain of work, so I let it sit there looking like a terrible mess. \n\nAnd it ain't just the yard work, neither. I sing in the church choir. Have for years and years, right there in the bass section. We practice on Thursday evenings in the fellowship hall, and we sing on Sunday mornings. But I have missed I don't know how many choir rehearsals lately. I will get my good shoes out, and then I just sit on the edge of the bed and I cannot get myself moving. It just feels like too much to get in the car and go down there and stand around with everybody. \n\nAnd then... well, I told you earlier about the fellas down at the community center, right? The ones I play dominoes with? Well, with all those little messages they were slipping into the conversation, me trying to figure out what they were warning me about and all... I reckon I started acting a bit strange about it, and I made the games mighty uncomfortable for everybody. I would be asking them what they meant by this or that, or I'd just stare at them waiting for the next clue, and they would get real quiet, just staring down at their tiles like they didn't know what to do with me. The whole atmosphere just got thick and awkward, you know? So... I do not join in like I used to. I haven't been down there to play in a good while now. It is a shame to lose out on the fellowship, but it is just easier to stay put in my armchair than to try and manage it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that your sense of how clear your head is, or your ability to keep track of the time of day, seems to get much better at some times and much worse at others?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I can't rightly say I've noticed anything like that. My head... well, it's about as clear as an old man's head can be, I suppose. It don't really come and go in spells like you're talking about, where one minute I'm sharp as a tack and the next I'm in a fog. It's just... well, it's just a steady sort of thing. \n\nNow, as for the time of day, I've always been a clock-watcher, ever since I was a young man working down at the mill. You had to know when the whistle was going to blow, you see. And I still keep a pretty good eye on the clock. My wife, she bought me one of those big digital ones for the nightstand a few Christmases back, the kind with the red numbers that glow in the dark, because she said I was always asking her what time it was when we'd wake up in the middle of the night. It's got big numbers, easy to read even without my glasses. And then there's the grandfather clock out in the hall—the one my daddy built. It chimes on the hour, every hour, and on the half-hour too. It's got a real deep, resonant sound, you know, kind of a 'bong, bong' that you can feel right in your chest if you're standing close enough. So between the digital clock and Daddy's clock, I always know exactly what time it is, morning, noon, or night. I never find myself wondering if it's breakfast time or supper time, or anything like that. I might get a little distracted, like I told you about with the church paper, but I don't lose track of the hours passing. It's just... well, I don't have those ups and downs with my thinking that you're asking about. It's all just pretty much the same, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the last few years, before these messages from the domino players started, would you say your memory or ability to think clearly was already starting to slip, or did these changes all seem to begin around the same time?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, if I really stop and chew on it, looking back over the years... no, it wasn't happening before. It all seemed to come on together, like a big dark storm rolling in all at once. I can pinpoint it pretty exact for you, actually. It's been going on for right about a year and five months now. Yes, sir, a year and five months. \n\nYou see, that was right after my close friend passed away. My buddy, Charlie. Charlie and me, we had known each other since we were just boys in knee pants. We worked down at the mill together all those years, went fishing down at the creek on our days off... he was actually the one who taught me how to play dominoes in the first place, if you can believe it. We played thousands of games together over the decades. Well, Charlie got older, same as me, and he took a bad turn and he passed on. We laid him to rest on a Thursday. I remember it clear as day because it was raining pitchforks and hammer handles, and my wife kept fussing at me to button up my good wool coat so I wouldn't catch my death out there in the graveyard mud. \n\nAnd it was right after that—right after we put Charlie in the ground—that all this strange business started up. Before that, my mind was doing just fine. I could remember what I was doing, I kept my vegetable garden neat as a pin, and I could just sit and play a regular game of dominoes without anyone slipping me any of those secret warnings or looking at me sideways. But after Charlie died, about a year and five months ago, that is exactly when the fog started rolling into my head. That's when I started losing my train of thought mid-sentence, getting pulled away by a bird or a car horn, and that's exactly when those other fellas at the community center started with their messages. \n\nIt is almost like Charlie leaving this earth just knocked something completely loose in my own life, you know what I mean? Like a wheel coming off a wagon. So, no, my thinking wasn't slipping before that. I was doing just fine. It all started right together, a year and five months ago, right after I lost my oldest friend. It’s just been one long, confusing stretch ever since we said goodbye to him.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend Charlie passed away, have you been feeling a persistent sense of sadness, hopelessness, or a lack of interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I mean... naturally, I miss Charlie. You don't know a fella since you were both running around in knee pants and not feel a great big hole when he’s gone. But as for feeling sad all day, every day, or feeling hopeless... no, I wouldn't say that at all. I don't sit around crying or feeling like the world is coming to an end. My wife, she actually asked me the very same thing not too long ago. She stood right there in the kitchen with a dishtowel in her hands and said, 'Are you depressed, old man?' And I told her, 'No, Martha, I'm not depressed.' \n\nIt’s not a dark cloud of sadness, you see. I remember when my own daddy passed away, back in the winter of '62, now *that* was sadness. I didn't eat a bite of supper for a week, just sat in the dark. But this... this is different. I know Charlie is in a better place, probably playing a round of dominoes with St. Peter himself, so I don't feel hopeless about it. I know the Good Lord still wakes me up every morning, and I'm thankful for it. I still like sitting on the back porch and feeling a nice breeze. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... well, it ain't that I don't *want* to enjoy them. Like my garden—I still care about my Beefsteak tomatoes. I still like the idea of them, and I still enjoy my hot cup of coffee in the morning looking out at the yard. If somebody brought me a plate of fried catfish and hushpuppies right now, I'd eat it and enjoy it just fine. It’s not that I don't care anymore, it's just... well, like I was telling you before, the gumption is just completely gone. The energy to get up and actually *do* the things, to get out there with the trowel and pull the crabgrass, or to go put on my good shoes and stand with the bass section in the choir... that’s what’s missing. \n\nIt just feels like a mountain of effort, especially with my head getting all foggy and those fellas down at the community center making things so thick and awkward with their secret messages. It takes a lot out of a man, trying to figure all that out. So I just stay put in my armchair. It’s just easier that way. But no, sir, I wouldn't call it hopeless or sad. Just... slowed down, I reckon. Just staying out of the way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes, sir, a year and five months. \n\nYou see, that was right after my close friend passed away. My buddy, Charlie. Charlie and me, we had known each other since we were just boys in knee pants. We worked down at the mill together all those years, went fishing down at the creek on our days off... he was actually the one who taught me how to play dominoes in the first place, if you can believe it. We played thousands of games together over the decades. Well, Charlie got older, same as me, and he took a bad turn and he passed on. We laid him to rest on a Thursday. I remember it clear as day because it was raining pitchforks and hammer handles, and my wife kept fussing at me to button up my good wool coat so I wouldn't catch my death out there in the graveyard mud. \n\nAnd it was right after that—right after we put Charlie in the ground—that all this strange business started up. Before that, my mind was doing just fine. I could remember what I was doing, I kept my vegetable garden neat as a pin, and I could just sit and play a regular game of dominoes without anyone slipping me any of those secret warnings or looking at me sideways. But after Charlie died, about a year and five months ago, that is exactly when the fog started rolling into my head. That's when I started losing my train of thought mid-sentence, getting pulled away by a bird or a car horn, and that's exactly when those other fellas at the community center started with their messages. \n\nIt is almost like Charlie leaving this earth just knocked something completely loose in my own life, you know what I mean? Like a wheel coming off a wagon. So, no, my thinking wasn't slipping before that. I was doing just fine. It all started right together, a year and five months ago, right after I lost my oldest friend. It’s just been one long, confusing stretch ever since we said goodbye to him.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend Charlie passed away, have you been feeling a persistent sense of sadness, hopelessness, or a lack of interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I mean... naturally, I miss Charlie. You don't know a fella since you were both running around in knee pants and not feel a great big hole when he’s gone. But as for feeling sad all day, every day, or feeling hopeless... no, I wouldn't say that at all. I don't sit around crying or feeling like the world is coming to an end. My wife, she actually asked me the very same thing not too long ago. She stood right there in the kitchen with a dishtowel in her hands and said, 'Are you depressed, old man?' And I told her, 'No, Martha, I'm not depressed.' \n\nIt’s not a dark cloud of sadness, you see. I remember when my own daddy passed away, back in the winter of '62, now *that* was sadness. I didn't eat a bite of supper for a week, just sat in the dark. But this... this is different. I know Charlie is in a better place, probably playing a round of dominoes with St. Peter himself, so I don't feel hopeless about it. I know the Good Lord still wakes me up every morning, and I'm thankful for it. I still like sitting on the back porch and feeling a nice breeze. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... well, it ain't that I don't *want* to enjoy them. Like my garden—I still care about my Beefsteak tomatoes. I still like the idea of them, and I still enjoy my hot cup of coffee in the morning looking out at the yard. If somebody brought me a plate of fried catfish and hushpuppies right now, I'd eat it and enjoy it just fine. It’s not that I don't care anymore, it's just... well, like I was telling you before, the gumption is just completely gone. The energy to get up and actually *do* the things, to get out there with the trowel and pull the crabgrass, or to go put on my good shoes and stand with the bass section in the choir... that’s what’s missing. \n\nIt just feels like a mountain of effort, especially with my head getting all foggy and those fellas down at the community center making things so thick and awkward with their secret messages. It takes a lot out of a man, trying to figure all that out. So I just stay put in my armchair. It’s just easier that way. But no, sir, I wouldn't call it hopeless or sad. Just... slowed down, I reckon. Just staying out of the way.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from the trouble with your focus and the messages you hear, have you noticed any changes in your physical coordination, such as having more trouble walking, feeling more unsteady on your feet, or experiencing any new tremors in your hands?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 84 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Doc, I can't rightly say I've had any trouble like that at all. My hands are about as steady as they've ever been. Now, if you look at 'em, sure, they got some thick joints and they look a bit like old tree roots from all those decades pulling heavy levers and handling rough timber down at the mill. But they don't shake, no sir. I can still pour my morning coffee right up to the very brim of the mug without spilling a single drop on Martha's good floral tablecloth. And let me tell you, she would have my hide hanging on the clothesline if I started staining up her linens. So no, no tremors or shaking or anything of that sort. 

And as for my walking, well, I ain't running any foot races these days, being eighty-four years old and all. My knees do pop a little bit when I first push up out of my armchair—sounds a bit like a bowl of dry cereal cracking open—but once I'm up on my feet, I'm solid. I walk out to the mailbox at the end of the drive every single day. I usually go right around eleven-thirty, just after the mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, drives by in his little white truck. It's a good sixty yards down our driveway, and it's got a slight uphill grade on the way back to the porch, but I don't wobble or weave around, and I certainly don't feel like I'm going to tip over into the azalea bushes. I just take my time, put one foot in front of the other, nice and easy. 

I've always had a real good sense of balance, you know. Even when I was just a young boy in knee pants, Charlie and me used to walk the steel rails on the train tracks down by the creek, seeing who could go the farthest without slipping off into the gravel, and I hardly ever fell. I guess I still got a bit of that balance left in me. So no, sir, my legs hold me up just fine, and my hands do exactly what I tell 'em to do. It's really just my mind that gets a little tangled up in the fog and with those fellas sending their signals down at the center. But the body? Well, the body is holding up pretty good for an old jalopy.
=====================================

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F06.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello. I'm Dr. Smith. To get us started, could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. Smith, it is... well, it is my wife who mostly said I should come down here and talk to somebody, but I suppose I have been feeling a bit on edge lately. You see, I go down to the community center to play dominoes with the fellas. Have been for years and years, ever since I got too old to do much else. And lately, the men at the domino table, they have been giving me these little messages. Not right out in the open, mind you, just tucked into the regular talk about the game or the weather, but I know they are meant for me. I can tell by the way things get quiet for a second. \n\nAnd it is not just them doing it, either. Even when I am sitting at home in my armchair, just resting my eyes and listening to the radio, the announcer does it too. He will be talking about the local news or the ballgame, and suddenly he will slip in a word or two that is meant just for my ears. My wife, she just waves her hand at me. She tells me it is just talk and I am getting worked up over nothing, but I know better. I have been around a long time, Doc. I know when people are trying to tell me something. They are warning me to be careful. I do not rightly know what I need to be careful of just yet, but a man does not get all these warnings from the radio and the domino fellas for nothing, you know? So I am just trying to keep my eyes open, but she just will not listen to me about it, so she made me come see you.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these messages you're hearing, have you been experiencing any other unusual sensations, like hearing voices when no one is around or seeing things that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you ask about it... I don't see anything that isn't there. My eyes aren't what they used to be anyway, so I wouldn't trust them much regardless. But hearing things... well, I suppose there is something. It happens mostly in the afternoons. You see, my wife, she likes to watch her television programs after lunch, or sometimes she goes over to her sister's place to help with the grandkids, so the house gets real, real quiet. Nothing but the sound of the refrigerator running or that old clock ticking in the hallway. \n\nAnd when it gets quiet like that, I... well, I hear my old friend. He passed on a few years back, God rest him, but I swear to you, Doc, I hear him out on the front porch. Clear as a bell. He'll call out my name, just exactly the way he used to when he'd come over to see if I wanted to sit a spell or walk down to the hardware store. Just calls my name right through the screen door. \n\nAnd if he ain't doing that, he's humming. He used to sing in the church choir—had this real deep, nice voice, you know, always stood in the back row on Sundays—and I will hear him humming one of those old choir songs out there. It is so clear, so loud and real, it is exactly like he is standing right there on the porch waiting on me. I even get up out of my chair sometimes, when my knees aren't bothering me too bad, and I go look out the front window. I'll pull the curtain back just a peek, fully expecting to see him leaning against the wooden railing in his good hat. But there is never anybody out there. Just the empty porch and the street. It is a peculiar thing, hearing him so plain like that when the house is quiet, but it is just so clear...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recent changes in your memory, your ability to focus, or your ability to keep track of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, my memory ain't what it used to be, I'll grant you that, but I'm 84 years old, so I reckon that's to be expected, ain't it? But as for focusing... well, that's a whole other kettle of fish lately. It's like my mind just can't sit still, no matter how hard I try to make it behave. \n\nJust the other day, I was sitting in my armchair, trying to read the church paper. Pastor leaves it in the mailbox every Tuesday, and I like to read the announcements, see who's sick or who had a new grandbaby, you know how it is. So I'm sitting there, got my reading glasses on, and I'm trying to read this little article about the upcoming bake sale. And my wife, she's in the kitchen, making up a batch of iced tea, and I'm trying to tell her about the bake sale, but then... well, a car drives by outside. Not even a loud car, just a regular old sedan, but the sound of those tires on the pavement, it just grabs me. And I look up, and I'm watching the car go down the street, and then I see a blue jay land on the telephone wire. And I'm looking at that blue jay, thinking about how they're such mean birds, always chasing the smaller ones away from the feeder, and then the radio in the kitchen catches my ear. It's just playing a commercial for that furniture store over in the next town, the one with the catchy little jingle, and suddenly I'm humming the jingle. \n\nAnd my wife, she comes in with the iced tea, and she says, \"Well, what about the bake sale?\" And I just look at her, Doc. I look right at her, and I have absolutely no earthly idea what she's talking about. I'd completely forgotten the church paper was even in my lap. I forgot I was reading it, I forgot I was talking to her... it's like my brain just gets pulled this way and that way, pulled by every little sound or movement, like a leaf in a strong wind. A bird, a car, a song on the radio... it just yanks me right away from whatever I'm doing, and I can't for the life of me remember what I was about to say or do. It's been happening more and more lately, and it's frustrating, I tell you. It's mighty frustrating to not be able to hold a thought in your head long enough to finish a sentence.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any times, perhaps when you are doing your grocery shopping or managing your household bills, where you find it difficult to complete tasks that you used to handle easily?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, see, my wife handles the checkbook. She always has, ever since I was working full-time. She's just always been better with the numbers, making sure the light bill and the water bill get mailed off on time. And she usually does the grocery shopping on Wednesday mornings because that is when the Piggly Wiggly does the senior discount. But as for other things... things I used to do all on my own without even thinking twice about them... well, I suppose it has been getting a lot harder lately. \n\nTake my garden, for example. I have always kept a nice vegetable patch out in the backyard. Grown the best Beefsteak tomatoes in the neighborhood, if I do say so myself, along with bush beans, and cucumbers, and summer squash. I used to be out there every single morning, soon as the sun was up, with my trowel and my old straw hat, pulling up the crabgrass and checking the leaves for bugs. But this year... well, I have just let parts of it go completely. I will stand there at the kitchen sink with my coffee, looking out the window, and I can see the weeds growing up knee-high by the back fence where the cucumbers are supposed to be. And I know I ought to go out there and tend to it, but I just... I don't. I can't seem to get the gumption to do it. It just feels like a mountain of work, so I let it sit there looking like a terrible mess. \n\nAnd it ain't just the yard work, neither. I sing in the church choir. Have for years and years, right there in the bass section. We practice on Thursday evenings in the fellowship hall, and we sing on Sunday mornings. But I have missed I don't know how many choir rehearsals lately. I will get my good shoes out, and then I just sit on the edge of the bed and I cannot get myself moving. It just feels like too much to get in the car and go down there and stand around with everybody. \n\nAnd then... well, I told you earlier about the fellas down at the community center, right? The ones I play dominoes with? Well, with all those little messages they were slipping into the conversation, me trying to figure out what they were warning me about and all... I reckon I started acting a bit strange about it, and I made the games mighty uncomfortable for everybody. I would be asking them what they meant by this or that, or I'd just stare at them waiting for the next clue, and they would get real quiet, just staring down at their tiles like they didn't know what to do with me. The whole atmosphere just got thick and awkward, you know? So... I do not join in like I used to. I haven't been down there to play in a good while now. It is a shame to lose out on the fellowship, but it is just easier to stay put in my armchair than to try and manage it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that your sense of how clear your head is, or your ability to keep track of the time of day, seems to get much better at some times and much worse at others?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I can't rightly say I've noticed anything like that. My head... well, it's about as clear as an old man's head can be, I suppose. It don't really come and go in spells like you're talking about, where one minute I'm sharp as a tack and the next I'm in a fog. It's just... well, it's just a steady sort of thing. \n\nNow, as for the time of day, I've always been a clock-watcher, ever since I was a young man working down at the mill. You had to know when the whistle was going to blow, you see. And I still keep a pretty good eye on the clock. My wife, she bought me one of those big digital ones for the nightstand a few Christmases back, the kind with the red numbers that glow in the dark, because she said I was always asking her what time it was when we'd wake up in the middle of the night. It's got big numbers, easy to read even without my glasses. And then there's the grandfather clock out in the hall—the one my daddy built. It chimes on the hour, every hour, and on the half-hour too. It's got a real deep, resonant sound, you know, kind of a 'bong, bong' that you can feel right in your chest if you're standing close enough. So between the digital clock and Daddy's clock, I always know exactly what time it is, morning, noon, or night. I never find myself wondering if it's breakfast time or supper time, or anything like that. I might get a little distracted, like I told you about with the church paper, but I don't lose track of the hours passing. It's just... well, I don't have those ups and downs with my thinking that you're asking about. It's all just pretty much the same, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the last few years, before these messages from the domino players started, would you say your memory or ability to think clearly was already starting to slip, or did these changes all seem to begin around the same time?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, if I really stop and chew on it, looking back over the years... no, it wasn't happening before. It all seemed to come on together, like a big dark storm rolling in all at once. I can pinpoint it pretty exact for you, actually. It's been going on for right about a year and five months now. Yes, sir, a year and five months. \n\nYou see, that was right after my close friend passed away. My buddy, Charlie. Charlie and me, we had known each other since we were just boys in knee pants. We worked down at the mill together all those years, went fishing down at the creek on our days off... he was actually the one who taught me how to play dominoes in the first place, if you can believe it. Before that, my mind was doing just fine. I could remember what I was doing, I kept my vegetable garden neat as a pin, and I could just sit and play a regular game of dominoes without anyone slipping me any of those secret warnings or looking at me sideways. But after Charlie died, about a year and five months ago, that is exactly when the fog started rolling into my head. And I told her, 'No, Martha, I'm not depressed.' \n\nIt’s not a dark cloud of sadness, you see. I remember when my own daddy passed away, back in the winter of '62, now *that* was sadness. It is so clear, so loud and real, it is exactly like he is standing right there on the porch waiting on me. I even get up out of my chair sometimes, when my knees aren't bothering me too bad, and I go look out the front window. I'll pull the curtain back just a peek, fully expecting to see him leaning against the wooden railing in his good hat. But there is never anybody out there. Just the empty porch and the street. It is a peculiar thing, hearing him so plain like that when the house is quiet, but it is just so clear...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recent changes in your memory, your ability to focus, or your ability to keep track of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, my memory ain't what it used to be, I'll grant you that, but I'm 84 years old, so I reckon that's to be expected, ain't it? But as for focusing... well, that's a whole other kettle of fish lately. It's like my mind just can't sit still, no matter how hard I try to make it behave. \n\nJust the other day, I was sitting in my armchair, trying to read the church paper. Pastor leaves it in the mailbox every Tuesday, and I like to read the announcements, see who's sick or who had a new grandbaby, you know how it is. So I'm sitting there, got my reading glasses on, and I'm trying to read this little article about the upcoming bake sale. And my wife, she's in the kitchen, making up a batch of iced tea, and I'm trying to tell her about the bake sale, but then... well, a car drives by outside. Not even a loud car, just a regular old sedan, but the sound of those tires on the pavement, it just grabs me. And I look up, and I'm watching the car go down the street, and then I see a blue jay land on the telephone wire. And I'm looking at that blue jay, thinking about how they're such mean birds, always chasing the smaller ones away from the feeder, and then the radio in the kitchen catches my ear. It's just playing a commercial for that furniture store over in the next town, the one with the catchy little jingle, and suddenly I'm humming the jingle. \n\nAnd my wife, she comes in with the iced tea, and she says, \"Well, what about the bake sale?\" And I just look at her, Doc. I look right at her, and I have absolutely no earthly idea what she's talking about. I'd completely forgotten the church paper was even in my lap. I forgot I was reading it, I forgot I was talking to her... it's like my brain just gets pulled this way and that way, pulled by every little sound or movement, like a leaf in a strong wind. A bird, a car, a song on the radio... it just yanks me right away from whatever I'm doing, and I can't for the life of me remember what I was about to say or do. It's been happening more and more lately, and it's frustrating, I tell you. It's mighty frustrating to not be able to hold a thought in your head long enough to finish a sentence.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any times, perhaps when you are doing your grocery shopping or managing your household bills, where you find it difficult to complete tasks that you used to handle easily?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, see, my wife handles the checkbook. She always has, ever since I was working full-time. She's just always been better with the numbers, making sure the light bill and the water bill get mailed off on time. And she usually does the grocery shopping on Wednesday mornings because that is when the Piggly Wiggly does the senior discount. But as for other things... things I used to do all on my own without even thinking twice about them... well, I suppose it has been getting a lot harder lately. \n\nTake my garden, for example. I have always kept a nice vegetable patch out in the backyard. Grown the best Beefsteak tomatoes in the neighborhood, if I do say so myself, along with bush beans, and cucumbers, and summer squash. I used to be out there every single morning, soon as the sun was up, with my trowel and my old straw hat, pulling up the crabgrass and checking the leaves for bugs. But this year... well, I have just let parts of it go completely. I will stand there at the kitchen sink with my coffee, looking out the window, and I can see the weeds growing up knee-high by the back fence where the cucumbers are supposed to be. And I know I ought to go out there and tend to it, but I just... I don't. I can't seem to get the gumption to do it. It just feels like a mountain of work, so I let it sit there looking like a terrible mess. \n\nAnd it ain't just the yard work, neither. I sing in the church choir. Have for years and years, right there in the bass section. We practice on Thursday evenings in the fellowship hall, and we sing on Sunday mornings. But I have missed I don't know how many choir rehearsals lately. I will get my good shoes out, and then I just sit on the edge of the bed and I cannot get myself moving. It just feels like too much to get in the car and go down there and stand around with everybody. \n\nAnd then... well, I told you earlier about the fellas down at the community center, right? The ones I play dominoes with? Well, with all those little messages they were slipping into the conversation, me trying to figure out what they were warning me about and all... I reckon I started acting a bit strange about it, and I made the games mighty uncomfortable for everybody. I would be asking them what they meant by this or that, or I'd just stare at them waiting for the next clue, and they would get real quiet, just staring down at their tiles like they didn't know what to do with me. The whole atmosphere just got thick and awkward, you know? So... I do not join in like I used to. I haven't been down there to play in a good while now. It is a shame to lose out on the fellowship, but it is just easier to stay put in my armchair than to try and manage it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that your sense of how clear your head is, or your ability to keep track of the time of day, seems to get much better at some times and much worse at others?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I can't rightly say I've noticed anything like that. My head... well, it's about as clear as an old man's head can be, I suppose. It don't really come and go in spells like you're talking about, where one minute I'm sharp as a tack and the next I'm in a fog. It's just... well, it's just a steady sort of thing. \n\nNow, as for the time of day, I've always been a clock-watcher, ever since I was a young man working down at the mill. You had to know when the whistle was going to blow, you see. And I still keep a pretty good eye on the clock. My wife, she bought me one of those big digital ones for the nightstand a few Christmases back, the kind with the red numbers that glow in the dark, because she said I was always asking her what time it was when we'd wake up in the middle of the night. It's got big numbers, easy to read even without my glasses. And then there's the grandfather clock out in the hall—the one my daddy built. It chimes on the hour, every hour, and on the half-hour too. It's got a real deep, resonant sound, you know, kind of a 'bong, bong' that you can feel right in your chest if you're standing close enough. So between the digital clock and Daddy's clock, I always know exactly what time it is, morning, noon, or night. I never find myself wondering if it's breakfast time or supper time, or anything like that. I might get a little distracted, like I told you about with the church paper, but I don't lose track of the hours passing. It's just... well, I don't have those ups and downs with my thinking that you're asking about. It's all just pretty much the same, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the last few years, before these messages from the domino players started, would you say your memory or ability to think clearly was already starting to slip, or did these changes all seem to begin around the same time?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, if I really stop and chew on it, looking back over the years... no, it wasn't happening before. It all seemed to come on together, like a big dark storm rolling in all at once. I can pinpoint it pretty exact for you, actually. It's been going on for right about a year and five months now. Yes, sir, a year and five months. \n\nYou see, that was right after my close friend passed away. My buddy, Charlie. Charlie and me, we had known each other since we were just boys in knee pants. We worked down at the mill together all those years, went fishing down at the creek on our days off... he was actually the one who taught me how to play dominoes in the first place, if you can believe it. We played thousands of games together over the decades. Well, Charlie got older, same as me, and he took a bad turn and he passed on. We laid him to rest on a Thursday. I remember it clear as day because it was raining pitchforks and hammer handles, and my wife kept fussing at me to button up my good wool coat so I wouldn't catch my death out there in the graveyard mud. \n\nAnd it was right after that—right after we put Charlie in the ground—that all this strange business started up. Before that, my mind was doing just fine. I could remember what I was doing, I kept my vegetable garden neat as a pin, and I could just sit and play a regular game of dominoes without anyone slipping me any of those secret warnings or looking at me sideways. But after Charlie died, about a year and five months ago, that is exactly when the fog started rolling into my head. That's when I started losing my train of thought mid-sentence, getting pulled away by a bird or a car horn, and that's exactly when those other fellas at the community center started with their messages. \n\nIt is almost like Charlie leaving this earth just knocked something completely loose in my own life, you know what I mean? Like a wheel coming off a wagon. So, no, my thinking wasn't slipping before that. I was doing just fine. It all started right together, a year and five months ago, right after I lost my oldest friend. It’s just been one long, confusing stretch ever since we said goodbye to him.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend Charlie passed away, have you been feeling a persistent sense of sadness, hopelessness, or a lack of interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I mean... naturally, I miss Charlie. You don't know a fella since you were both running around in knee pants and not feel a great big hole when he’s gone. But as for feeling sad all day, every day, or feeling hopeless... no, I wouldn't say that at all. I don't sit around crying or feeling like the world is coming to an end. My wife, she actually asked me the very same thing not too long ago. She stood right there in the kitchen with a dishtowel in her hands and said, 'Are you depressed, old man?' And I told her, 'No, Martha, I'm not depressed.' \n\nIt’s not a dark cloud of sadness, you see. I remember when my own daddy passed away, back in the winter of '62, now *that* was sadness. I didn't eat a bite of supper for a week, just sat in the dark. But this... this is different. I know Charlie is in a better place, probably playing a round of dominoes with St. Peter himself, so I don't feel hopeless about it. I know the Good Lord still wakes me up every morning, and I'm thankful for it. I still like sitting on the back porch and feeling a nice breeze. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... well, it ain't that I don't *want* to enjoy them. Like my garden—I still care about my Beefsteak tomatoes. I still like the idea of them, and I still enjoy my hot cup of coffee in the morning looking out at the yard. If somebody brought me a plate of fried catfish and hushpuppies right now, I'd eat it and enjoy it just fine. It’s not that I don't care anymore, it's just... well, like I was telling you before, the gumption is just completely gone. The energy to get up and actually *do* the things, to get out there with the trowel and pull the crabgrass, or to go put on my good shoes and stand with the bass section in the choir... that’s what’s missing. \n\nIt just feels like a mountain of effort, especially with my head getting all foggy and those fellas down at the community center making things so thick and awkward with their secret messages. It takes a lot out of a man, trying to figure all that out. So I just stay put in my armchair. It’s just easier that way. But no, sir, I wouldn't call it hopeless or sad. Just... slowed down, I reckon. Just staying out of the way.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble with your focus and the messages you hear, have you noticed any changes in your physical coordination, such as having more trouble walking, feeling more unsteady on your feet, or experiencing any new tremors in your hands?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I can't rightly say I've had any trouble like that at all. My hands are about as steady as they've ever been. Now, if you look at 'em, sure, they got some thick joints and they look a bit like old tree roots from all those decades pulling heavy levers and handling rough timber down at the mill. But they don't shake, no sir. I can still pour my morning coffee right up to the very brim of the mug without spilling a single drop on Martha's good floral tablecloth. And let me tell you, she would have my hide hanging on the clothesline if I started staining up her linens. So no, no tremors or shaking or anything of that sort. \n\nAnd as for my walking, well, I ain't running any foot races these days, being eighty-four years old and all. My knees do pop a little bit when I first push up out of my armchair—sounds a bit like a bowl of dry cereal cracking open—but once I'm up on my feet, I'm solid. I walk out to the mailbox at the end of the drive every single day. I usually go right around eleven-thirty, just after the mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, drives by in his little white truck. It's a good sixty yards down our driveway, and it's got a slight uphill grade on the way back to the porch, but I don't wobble or weave around, and I certainly don't feel like I'm going to tip over into the azalea bushes. I just take my time, put one foot in front of the other, nice and easy. \n\nI've always had a real good sense of balance, you know. Even when I was just a young boy in knee pants, Charlie and me used to walk the steel rails on the train tracks down by the creek, seeing who could go the farthest without slipping off into the gravel, and I hardly ever fell. I guess I still got a bit of that balance left in me. So no, sir, my legs hold me up just fine, and my hands do exactly what I tell 'em to do. It's really just my mind that gets a little tangled up in the fog and with those fellas sending their signals down at the center. But the body? Well, the body is holding up pretty good for an old jalopy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

We worked down at the mill together all those years, went fishing down at the creek on our days off... he was actually the one who taught me how to play dominoes in the first place, if you can believe it. We played thousands of games together over the decades. Well, Charlie got older, same as me, and he took a bad turn and he passed on. We laid him to rest on a Thursday. I remember it clear as day because it was raining pitchforks and hammer handles, and my wife kept fussing at me to button up my good wool coat so I wouldn't catch my death out there in the graveyard mud. \n\nAnd it was right after that—right after we put Charlie in the ground—that all this strange business started up. Before that, my mind was doing just fine. I could remember what I was doing, I kept my vegetable garden neat as a pin, and I could just sit and play a regular game of dominoes without anyone slipping me any of those secret warnings or looking at me sideways. But after Charlie died, about a year and five months ago, that is exactly when the fog started rolling into my head. That's when I started losing my train of thought mid-sentence, getting pulled away by a bird or a car horn, and that's exactly when those other fellas at the community center started with their messages. \n\nIt is almost like Charlie leaving this earth just knocked something completely loose in my own life, you know what I mean? Like a wheel coming off a wagon. So, no, my thinking wasn't slipping before that. I was doing just fine. It all started right together, a year and five months ago, right after I lost my oldest friend. It’s just been one long, confusing stretch ever since we said goodbye to him.\n\nDoctor: Since your friend Charlie passed away, have you been feeling a persistent sense of sadness, hopelessness, or a lack of interest in the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I mean... naturally, I miss Charlie. You don't know a fella since you were both running around in knee pants and not feel a great big hole when he’s gone. But as for feeling sad all day, every day, or feeling hopeless... no, I wouldn't say that at all. I don't sit around crying or feeling like the world is coming to an end. My wife, she actually asked me the very same thing not too long ago. She stood right there in the kitchen with a dishtowel in her hands and said, 'Are you depressed, old man?' And I told her, 'No, Martha, I'm not depressed.' \n\nIt’s not a dark cloud of sadness, you see. I remember when my own daddy passed away, back in the winter of '62, now *that* was sadness. I didn't eat a bite of supper for a week, just sat in the dark. But this... this is different. I know Charlie is in a better place, probably playing a round of dominoes with St. Peter himself, so I don't feel hopeless about it. I know the Good Lord still wakes me up every morning, and I'm thankful for it. I still like sitting on the back porch and feeling a nice breeze. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... well, it ain't that I don't *want* to enjoy them. Like my garden—I still care about my Beefsteak tomatoes. I still like the idea of them, and I still enjoy my hot cup of coffee in the morning looking out at the yard. If somebody brought me a plate of fried catfish and hushpuppies right now, I'd eat it and enjoy it just fine. It’s not that I don't care anymore, it's just... well, like I was telling you before, the gumption is just completely gone. The energy to get up and actually *do* the things, to get out there with the trowel and pull the crabgrass, or to go put on my good shoes and stand with the bass section in the choir... that’s what’s missing. \n\nIt just feels like a mountain of effort, especially with my head getting all foggy and those fellas down at the community center making things so thick and awkward with their secret messages. It takes a lot out of a man, trying to figure all that out. So I just stay put in my armchair. It’s just easier that way. But no, sir, I wouldn't call it hopeless or sad. Just... slowed down, I reckon. Just staying out of the way.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble with your focus and the messages you hear, have you noticed any changes in your physical coordination, such as having more trouble walking, feeling more unsteady on your feet, or experiencing any new tremors in your hands?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I can't rightly say I've had any trouble like that at all. My hands are about as steady as they've ever been. Now, if you look at 'em, sure, they got some thick joints and they look a bit like old tree roots from all those decades pulling heavy levers and handling rough timber down at the mill. But they don't shake, no sir. I can still pour my morning coffee right up to the very brim of the mug without spilling a single drop on Martha's good floral tablecloth. And let me tell you, she would have my hide hanging on the clothesline if I started staining up her linens. So no, no tremors or shaking or anything of that sort. \n\nAnd as for my walking, well, I ain't running any foot races these days, being eighty-four years old and all. My knees do pop a little bit when I first push up out of my armchair—sounds a bit like a bowl of dry cereal cracking open—but once I'm up on my feet, I'm solid. I walk out to the mailbox at the end of the drive every single day. I usually go right around eleven-thirty, just after the mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, drives by in his little white truck. It's a good sixty yards down our driveway, and it's got a slight uphill grade on the way back to the porch, but I don't wobble or weave around, and I certainly don't feel like I'm going to tip over into the azalea bushes. I just take my time, put one foot in front of the other, nice and easy. \n\nI've always had a real good sense of balance, you know. Even when I was just a young boy in knee pants, Charlie and me used to walk the steel rails on the train tracks down by the creek, seeing who could go the farthest without slipping off into the gravel, and I hardly ever fell. I guess I still got a bit of that balance left in me. So no, sir, my legs hold me up just fine, and my hands do exactly what I tell 'em to do. It's really just my mind that gets a little tangled up in the fog and with those fellas sending their signals down at the center. But the body? Well, the body is holding up pretty good for an old jalopy.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you currently taking any prescription medications for any health conditions, or have you been diagnosed with any medical illnesses by another doctor recently?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 84 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Doc, if you can believe it, I don't take a single pill. Not a one. Now, my wife Martha, she’s got a whole row of those little orange plastic bottles lined up on the kitchen counter, right there next to the toaster and the ceramic rooster where she keeps the tea bags. She’s got one for her blood pressure, one for her water, and Lord knows what else, and she’s always shaking them at me, telling me I ought to go get a physical and see what’s rusting out on my own undercarriage. But I just tell her, Martha, if it ain't broke, don't take a pill to fix it. 

So no, sir, I don't take any prescription medications at all. I never really held with them. Back when Charlie and me were working the heavy saws down at the mill, if you got a scrape or an ache, you just rubbed a little horse liniment on it and went right back to work. We didn't have time to be running off to the apothecary for every little sniffle or sore joint. And as for any other doctors telling me I have a new illness or a condition or anything like that... well, I haven't even been to a regular medical doctor in I don't know how many years. 

I think the last fellow I saw for my body was old Dr. Higgins, and he retired and moved down to Florida to play golf way back in... oh, it must have been nineteen and ninety-six. Maybe ninety-seven. He looked down my throat with a wooden stick because I had a bad winter cough, told me to drink hot tea with honey, and that was the end of that. I just haven't had the need to go back to anybody since. I've always had a strong constitution, you see. My daddy was the exact same way, lived to be eighty-nine and never took a single pill except for an aspirin when he hit his thumb with a framing hammer. So no, no new illnesses, no prescriptions, none of that business. Just the fog in my head and those fellas at the center causing a ruckus, but nothing wrong with my actual health, no sir.

I'm Dr. Smith. To get us started, could you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. Smith, it is... well, it is my wife who mostly said I should come down here and talk to somebody, but I suppose I have been feeling a bit on edge lately. You see, I go down to the community center to play dominoes with the fellas. Have been for years and years, ever since I got too old to do much else. And lately, the men at the domino table, they have been giving me these little messages. Not right out in the open, mind you, just tucked into the regular talk about the game or the weather, but I know they are meant for me. I can tell by the way things get quiet for a second. \n\nAnd it is not just them doing it, either. Even when I am sitting at home in my armchair, just resting my eyes and listening to the radio, the announcer does it too. He will be talking about the local news or the ballgame, and suddenly he will slip in a word or two that is meant just for my ears. My wife, she just waves her hand at me. She tells me it is just talk and I am getting worked up over nothing, but I know better. I have been around a long time, Doc. I know when people are trying to tell me something. They are warning me to be careful. I do not rightly know what I need to be careful of just yet, but a man does not get all these warnings from the radio and the domino fellas for nothing, you know? So I am just trying to keep my eyes open, but she just will not listen to me about it, so she made me come see you.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these messages you're hearing, have you been experiencing any other unusual sensations, like hearing voices when no one is around or seeing things that others don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Well, now that you ask about it... I don't see anything that isn't there. My eyes aren't what they used to be anyway, so I wouldn't trust them much regardless. But hearing things... well, I suppose there is something. It happens mostly in the afternoons. Nothing but the sound of the refrigerator running or that old clock ticking in the hallway. \n\nAnd when it gets quiet like that, I... well, I hear my old friend. He passed on a few years back, God rest him, but I swear to you, Doc, I hear him out on the front porch. Clear as a bell. He'll call out my name, just exactly the way he used to when he'd come over to see if I wanted to sit a spell or walk down to the hardware store. Just calls my name right through the screen door. \n\nAnd if he ain't doing that, he's humming. He used to sing in the church choir—had this real deep, nice voice, you know, always stood in the back row on Sundays—and I will hear him humming one of those old choir songs out there. It is so clear, so loud and real, it is exactly like he is standing right there on the porch waiting on me. I even get up out of my chair sometimes, when my knees aren't bothering me too bad, and I go look out the front window. I'll pull the curtain back just a peek, fully expecting to see him leaning against the wooden railing in his good hat. But there is never anybody out there. Just the empty porch and the street. It is a peculiar thing, hearing him so plain like that when the house is quiet, but it is just so clear...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recent changes in your memory, your ability to focus, or your ability to keep track of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, my memory ain't what it used to be, I'll grant you that, but I'm 84 years old, so I reckon that's to be expected, ain't it? But as for focusing... well, that's a whole other kettle of fish lately. It's like my mind just can't sit still, no matter how hard I try to make it behave. \n\nJust the other day, I was sitting in my armchair, trying to read the church paper. Pastor leaves it in the mailbox every Tuesday, and I like to read the announcements, see who's sick or who had a new grandbaby, you know how it is. So I'm sitting there, got my reading glasses on, and I'm trying to read this little article about the upcoming bake sale. And my wife, she's in the kitchen, making up a batch of iced tea, and I'm trying to tell her about the bake sale, but then... well, a car drives by outside. Not even a loud car, just a regular old sedan, but the sound of those tires on the pavement, it just grabs me. And I look up, and I'm watching the car go down the street, and then I see a blue jay land on the telephone wire. And I'm looking at that blue jay, thinking about how they're such mean birds, always chasing the smaller ones away from the feeder, and then the radio in the kitchen catches my ear. It's just playing a commercial for that furniture store over in the next town, the one with the catchy little jingle, and suddenly I'm humming the jingle. \n\nAnd my wife, she comes in with the iced tea, and she says, \"Well, what about the bake sale?\" And I just look at her, Doc. I look right at her, and I have absolutely no earthly idea what she's talking about. I'd completely forgotten the church paper was even in my lap. I forgot I was reading it, I forgot I was talking to her... it's like my brain just gets pulled this way and that way, pulled by every little sound or movement, like a leaf in a strong wind. A bird, a car, a song on the radio... it just yanks me right away from whatever I'm doing, and I can't for the life of me remember what I was about to say or do. It's been happening more and more lately, and it's frustrating, I tell you. It's mighty frustrating to not be able to hold a thought in your head long enough to finish a sentence.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any times, perhaps when you are doing your grocery shopping or managing your household bills, where you find it difficult to complete tasks that you used to handle easily?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, see, my wife handles the checkbook. She always has, ever since I was working full-time. She's just always been better with the numbers, making sure the light bill and the water bill get mailed off on time. And she usually does the grocery shopping on Wednesday mornings because that is when the Piggly Wiggly does the senior discount. But as for other things... things I used to do all on my own without even thinking twice about them... well, I suppose it has been getting a lot harder lately. \n\nTake my garden, for example. I have always kept a nice vegetable patch out in the backyard. Grown the best Beefsteak tomatoes in the neighborhood, if I do say so myself, along with bush beans, and cucumbers, and summer squash. I used to be out there every single morning, soon as the sun was up, with my trowel and my old straw hat, pulling up the crabgrass and checking the leaves for bugs. But this year... well, I have just let parts of it go completely. I will stand there at the kitchen sink with my coffee, looking out the window, and I can see the weeds growing up knee-high by the back fence where the cucumbers are supposed to be. And I know I ought to go out there and tend to it, but I just... I don't. I can't seem to get the gumption to do it. It just feels like a mountain of work, so I let it sit there looking like a terrible mess. \n\nAnd it ain't just the yard work, neither. I sing in the church choir. Have for years and years, right there in the bass section. We practice on Thursday evenings in the fellowship hall, and we sing on Sunday mornings. But I have missed I don't know how many choir rehearsals lately. I will get my good shoes out, and then I just sit on the edge of the bed and I cannot get myself moving. It just feels like too much to get in the car and go down there and stand around with everybody. \n\nAnd then... well, I told you earlier about the fellas down at the community center, right? The ones I play dominoes with? Well, with all those little messages they were slipping into the conversation, me trying to figure out what they were warning me about and all... I reckon I started acting a bit strange about it, and I made the games mighty uncomfortable for everybody. I would be asking them what they meant by this or that, or I'd just stare at them waiting for the next clue, and they would get real quiet, just staring down at their tiles like they didn't know what to do with me. The whole atmosphere just got thick and awkward, you know? So... I do not join in like I used to. I haven't been down there to play in a good while now. It is a shame to lose out on the fellowship, but it is just easier to stay put in my armchair than to try and manage it all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that your sense of how clear your head is, or your ability to keep track of the time of day, seems to get much better at some times and much worse at others?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I can't rightly say I've noticed anything like that. My head... well, it's about as clear as an old man's head can be, I suppose. It don't really come and go in spells like you're talking about, where one minute I'm sharp as a tack and the next I'm in a fog. It's just... well, it's just a steady sort of thing. \n\nNow, as for the time of day, I've always been a clock-watcher, ever since I was a young man working down at the mill. You had to know when the whistle was going to blow, you see. And I still keep a pretty good eye on the clock. My wife, she bought me one of those big digital ones for the nightstand a few Christmases back, the kind with the red numbers that glow in the dark, because she said I was always asking her what time it was when we'd wake up in the middle of the night. It's got big numbers, easy to read even without my glasses. And then there's the grandfather clock out in the hall—the one my daddy built. It chimes on the hour, every hour, and on the half-hour too. It's got a real deep, resonant sound, you know, kind of a 'bong, bong' that you can feel right in your chest if you're standing close enough. I never find myself wondering if it's breakfast time or supper time, or anything like that.
